LFT is supported by a Dalitz Scholarship from the University of Oxford and Wadham College.
For the purpose of open access, the authors have applied a CC BY public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.

Appendix A
Potential for the tensor model

The full analytic expression for the potential at optimal
N
scaling
[
52
]
is as follows.
We have normalised each tensor term such that it has overall weight one, i.e. if
N=1
, then
V={{\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\over 2}}\phi^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi+{{\sum_{i}h_{i}\over 6!}}\phi^{6}
.
For compactness, we present the equations that are not symmetrised (with weight one) under permutation of the bosonic indices; to use these formulae, a full calculation the symmetrised versions must be used
14
14

14

This symmetrisation is straightforward in each case: on one example
\lambda
term, we do
r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}\mapsto{{1\over 2}}(r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+(i\leftrightarrow j))
; on an example
h
term,
r_{j}^{i}r_{m}^{l}r_{n}^{k}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\mapsto{{1\over 6!}}(\text{same}+(6!-1)\text{ perms})
.
. We also use as shorthand
r^{i}_{j}=\delta_{i_{r}j_{r}}
(likewise for
g
and
b
).

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\lambda_{(i_{r}i_{g}i_{b})(j_{r}j_{g}j_{b})(k_{r}k_{g}k_{b})(l_{r}l_{g}l_{b})}=\Big[

(68)

\displaystyle+{{\lambda_{t}\over 3N^{3/2}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{l}^{k}b_{k}^{i}b_{l}^{j}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{k}^{i}r_{l}^{j}b_{j}^{i}b_{l}^{k}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{k}^{i}r_{l}^{j}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{j}^{i}g_{l}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle+{{\lambda_{\text{dD}}\over N^{3}}}\left(r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{\lambda_{\text{dS}}\over N^{3}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{l}^{k}b_{j}^{i}b_{l}^{k}g_{j}^{i}g_{l}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle+{{\lambda_{\text{pE}}\over 3N^{2}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{l}^{k}b_{j}^{i}b_{l}^{k}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{j}^{i}r_{l}^{k}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{j}^{i}g_{l}^{k}+r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{j}^{i}b_{l}^{k}g_{j}^{i}g_{l}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle+{{\lambda_{\text{pO}}\over 3N^{2}}}\left(r_{k}^{i}r_{l}^{j}b_{k}^{i}b_{l}^{j}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{k}^{i}r_{l}^{j}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{k}^{i}b_{l}^{j}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{\lambda_{\text{pS}}\over 3N^{2}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{l}^{k}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{j}^{i}b_{l}^{k}g_{k}^{j}g_{l}^{i}+r_{k}^{j}r_{l}^{i}b_{k}^{j}b_{l}^{i}g_{j}^{i}g_{l}^{k}\right)\Big]_{\text{symmetrised on $i\leftrightarrow j$}}

\displaystyle h_{(j_{r}j_{g}j_{b})(k_{r}k_{g}k_{b})(l_{r}l_{g}l_{b})(m_{r}m_{g}m_{b})(n_{r}n_{g}n_{b})}=\Big[

\displaystyle+{{h_{1}\over N^{3}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{m}^{l}r_{n}^{k}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{2}\over N^{3}}}\left(r_{l}^{j}r_{m}^{i}r_{n}^{k}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{3}\over 3N^{7/2}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{m}^{l}r_{n}^{k}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{k}^{i}r_{m}^{l}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{k}^{j}r_{m}^{l}r_{n}^{i}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{4}\over 3N^{4}}}\left(r_{l}^{i}r_{m}^{k}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{l}^{i}r_{m}^{k}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{j}b_{n}^{i}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{l}^{k}r_{m}^{j}r_{n}^{i}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{5}\over 3N^{4}}}\left(r_{l}^{i}r_{m}^{k}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{l}^{k}r_{m}^{i}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{i}b_{m}^{k}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{l}^{j}r_{m}^{k}r_{n}^{i}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{6}\over N^{9/2}}}\left(r_{j}^{i}r_{l}^{k}r_{n}^{m}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{7}\over 3N^{5}}}\left(r_{l}^{k}r_{m}^{i}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{l}^{k}r_{m}^{i}r_{n}^{j}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{j}b_{n}^{i}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}+r_{l}^{k}r_{m}^{j}r_{n}^{i}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{i}b_{n}^{j}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+{{h_{8}\over N^{6}}}\left(r_{l}^{k}r_{m}^{j}r_{n}^{i}b_{l}^{k}b_{m}^{j}b_{n}^{i}g_{l}^{k}g_{m}^{j}g_{n}^{i}\right)\Big]_{\text{symmetrised on $S_{6}$}}

The unpleasantness of the notation should make manifest the reason for the visual shorthand.

A.1
Potential comparison

To ease comparison with the other sextic models of the literature, we directly compare our real bosonic potential sector,
\{h_{i}\}
, to the
complex
bosonic sextic potential in
[
5
]
(
\{\bm{\lambda}_{i}(\bar{\phi}\phi)^{3}\}
), and the real bosonic sextic potential in
[
8
]
(
\{\bm{g}_{i}\phi^{6}\}
).

If we make the fermion sector free (
\lambda_{i}=0
), and restrict to the couplings that descend from
\mathrm{U}(N)
-invariant couplings (see
A
), we obtain from our beta functions exactly the large-
N
results of the complex sextic melonic model, up to a rescaling
h=15\bm{\lambda}/s^{3}
. This is despite their model being complex, and so in theory having double the number of degrees of freedom.

Since all
O\in\mathrm{O}(N)
also satisfy
O\in\mathrm{U}(N)
, as
O^{\dagger}=O^{T}=O^{-1}
, we note that all possible
\mathrm{U}(N)^{3}
interaction terms are manifestly also invariant under
\mathrm{O}(N)^{3}
. Thus, the
\mathrm{U}(N)^{3}
interaction terms are a subset of the
\mathrm{O}(N)^{3}
interaction terms, so we can identify:

Real sextic

Complex sextic
(\bar{\phi}\phi)^{3}

Real prismatic
\phi^{6}

h_{p}/N^{3}

Forbidden by
\mathrm{U}(N)

\bm{g}_{1}/N^{3}

h_{w}/N^{3}

\bm{\lambda}_{1}/N^{3}

\bm{g}_{2}/N^{5}

h_{3}/N^{7/2}

Forbidden by
\mathrm{U}(N)

\bm{g}_{5}/N^{4}

h_{4}/N^{4}

\bm{\lambda}_{3}/N^{4}

\bm{g}_{4}/N^{5}

h_{5}/N^{4}

\bm{\lambda}_{2}/N^{4}

\bm{g}_{3}/N^{4}

h_{6}/N^{9/2}

Forbidden by
\mathrm{U}(N)

\bm{g}_{6}/N^{5}

h_{7}/N^{5}

\bm{\lambda}_{4}/N^{5}

\bm{g}_{7}/N^{5}

h_{8}/N^{6}

\bm{\lambda}_{5}/N^{6}

\bm{h}_{8}/N^{7}

The prismatic models have picked non-optimal scalings and therefore miss some physics.

Appendix B
Perturbative beta functions

B.1
Notes on the calculation

B.1.1
Gamma matrices in 2+1D

Our particles here are 3D Dirac spinors, with 2 complex, and so 4 real, components. The gamma matrices in
(2+1)D
, with signature
(-++)
, can be the Pauli matrices, with up to a factor of
i
for
\gamma^{0}
:

\gamma^{0}=i\sigma_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&i\\
i&0\\
\end{array}\right),\gamma^{1}=\sigma_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-i\\
i&0\\
\end{array}\right),\gamma^{2}=\sigma_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{array}\right)

(69)

In precisely three dimensions, the trace rules are the following:

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\gamma^{\mu}]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}]=2\eta^{\mu\nu},

(70)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\rho}]

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho},\qquad\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\text{ s.t. }\epsilon^{012}=+1,

with
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}
the alternating tensor with the normalisation indicated. However, it is well known that DReD is algebraically inconsistent; in particular, because different contractions of three or more
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}
factors yield different results in
D<3

[
74
]
. Our computation of the beta functions in
D=3-\epsilon
did not suffer from this as we have no more than one momentum at any point in our computation (as we renormalised the couplings at zero momentum), and thus no epsilon factors can ever occur, because we can always reduce any trace using the identity
\not{p}\not{p}=p^{2}
. Thus, as long as we take traces only at the end, no problems can arise.

B.1.2
Massive melonic integrals in the epsilon expansion

We give here the fermionic sunrise integral, in full generality of masses and momenta, along with conventions. The standard Lorentzian sunrise integral, with
(+--)
conventions, in
D=3-\epsilon
can be found in the literature
[
75
]
:

\begin{split}&\mu^{2\epsilon}\int{{\mathrm{d}^{D}k\,\mathrm{d}^{D}l\over(2\pi)^{2D}}}\left({{1\over(k^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\eta)(l^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+i\eta)(k+l-p)^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\eta)}}\right),\\
&{{1\over 32\pi^{2}}}\left({{1\over\epsilon}}+\log\left({{\bar{\mu}^{2}\over M^{2}-p^{2}}}\right)-{{2M\tanh^{-1}\left({{p+0i\over M}}\right)\over p}}+3+O(\epsilon^{1})\right)\end{split}

(71)

where we have defined the usual
\overline{MS}
parameter,
\bar{\mu}^{2}=e^{-\gamma_{E}}4\pi\mu^{2}
, and the mass sum
M\equiv m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
. Next, including fermions:

\begin{split}&\mu^{2\epsilon}\int{{\mathrm{d}^{D}k\,\mathrm{d}^{D}l\over(2\pi)^{2D}}}\left({{k\cdot l\over(k^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\eta)(l^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+i\eta)(k+l-p)^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\eta)}}\right)\\
&={{1\over 192\pi^{2}}}\Biggl({{p^{2}-3\left(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}\right)\over\epsilon}}+\left(p^{2}-3(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})\right)\log\left({{\bar{\mu}^{2}\over M^{2}-p^{2}}}\right)\\
&\quad+{{2\left(3m_{3}\left(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-p^{2}\right)-m_{3}^{3}+2\left(m_{1}^{3}+m_{2}^{3}\right)\right)\tanh^{-1}\left({{p+0i\over M}}\right)\over p}}\\
&\quad-7m_{1}^{2}-7m_{2}^{2}+5m_{3}^{2}-2m_{1}m_{2}+4\left(m_{1}+m_{2}\right)m_{3}+3p^{2}+O(\epsilon^{1})\Biggr)\end{split}

(72)

and

\begin{split}&\mu^{2\epsilon}\int{{\mathrm{d}^{D}k\,\mathrm{d}^{D}l\over(2\pi)^{2D}}}\left({{k\cdot p\over(k^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\eta)(l^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+i\eta)(k+l-p)^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\eta)}}\right),\\
&={{1\over 96\pi^{2}}}\Biggl({{p^{2}\over\epsilon}}+p^{2}\log\left({{\bar{\mu}^{2}\over M^{2}-p^{2}}}\right)\\
&\quad+{{\left(\left(m_{2}+m_{3}\right)^{3}-3\left(m_{2}+m_{3}\right)p^{2}-2m_{1}^{3}-3\left(m_{2}+m_{3}\right)m_{1}^{2}\right)\tanh^{-1}\left({{p+0i\over M}}\right)\over p}}\\
&\quad+\left(2m_{1}-m_{2}-m_{3}\right)M+3p^{2}+O(\epsilon^{1})\Biggr)\end{split}

(73)

We have specified the branch of
\mathrm{arctanh}
that we take for
p=\sqrt{p^{2}}>M=m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
. All divergent
2
-loop integrals in
3-\epsilon
dimensions can be obtained from variations on this theme, although integration by parts identities may be required for the more non-trivial ones (say, with a numerator of
(k\cdot p)(l\cdot p)
).

B.2
Full beta functions

We present the full beta functions for the tensorial
\phi^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi
model at leading order in
N
, with all marginal couplings set to zero. We use
s=1/(8\pi)
to indicate the order calculated to, since each
\lambda
comes with an
s
and each
h
comes with an
s^{2}
. Plugging (
68
) into (
12
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\beta\left(\lambda_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\lambda_{t}+{{1\over 9}}s^{2}(T+1)\lambda_{t}^{3}+s^{4}\left(\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 16200}}\right)\lambda_{t}+{{1\over 486}}(-T(11T+26)-2)\lambda_{t}^{5}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(\lambda_{d_{S}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\lambda_{d_{S}}+{{2\over 9}}s^{2}(T+1)\lambda_{t}^{2}\left(\lambda_{p_{E}}+2\lambda_{d_{S}}\right)

\displaystyle+s^{4}\left(\left(h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\right){{3\lambda_{p_{E}}+4\lambda_{d_{S}}\over 4050}}-{{2\over 243}}\lambda_{t}^{4}\left(18\lambda_{p_{E}}+23\lambda_{d_{S}}+5T^{2}\lambda_{d_{S}}+56T\lambda_{d_{S}}+39\lambda_{p_{E}}T\right)\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(\lambda_{d_{D}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\lambda_{d_{D}}+{{1\over 9}}s^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\left((T+7)\lambda_{d_{D}}+4\left(\lambda_{p_{O}}+\lambda_{p_{S}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+{{s^{4}\over 48600}}\left(\begin{multlined}(3h_{p}^{2}+9h_{w}^{2}-540h_{p}\lambda_{t}^{2}-11000\lambda_{t}^{4}-1100T^{2}\lambda_{t}^{4}-11600T\lambda_{t}^{4})\lambda_{d_{D}}\\
\\
-(480h_{p}\lambda_{t}^{2}+8400\lambda_{t}^{4}+6000T\lambda_{t}^{4})(\lambda_{p_{O}}+\lambda_{p_{S}})\end{multlined}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(\lambda_{p_{E}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{p_{E}}\epsilon+{{2\over 9}}\lambda_{p_{E}}s^{2}(T+1)\lambda_{t}^{2}+s^{4}\left({{h_{p}^{2}\lambda_{p_{E}}\over 4050}}+{{h_{w}^{2}\lambda_{p_{E}}\over 1350}}-{{2\over 243}}\lambda_{p_{E}}(T(5T+17)+5)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(\lambda_{p_{O}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\lambda_{p_{O}}+{{1\over 9}}s^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\left(\lambda_{p_{O}}(1+T)+2\lambda_{p_{S}}\right)

\displaystyle+s^{4}\left({{\left(h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\right)\lambda_{p_{O}}\over 16200}}-{{1\over 810}}h_{p}\lambda_{p_{O}}\lambda_{t}^{2}-{{1\over 486}}\lambda_{t}^{4}\left((T+2)(11T+4)\lambda_{p_{O}}+6(5T+3)\lambda_{p_{S}}\right)\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(\lambda_{p_{S}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\lambda_{p_{S}}+{{1\over 9}}s^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\left(\lambda_{p_{S}}(1+T)+2\lambda_{p_{O}}\right)

\displaystyle+s^{4}\left({{\left(h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\right)\lambda_{p_{S}}\over 16200}}-{{1\over 810}}h_{p}\lambda_{p_{S}}\lambda_{t}^{2}-{{1\over 486}}\lambda_{t}^{4}\left((T+2)(11T+4)\lambda_{p_{S}}+6(5T+3)\lambda_{p_{O}}\right)\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{p}+s^{2}\left({{1\over 3}}h_{p}T\lambda_{t}^{2}+{{h_{p}^{2}\over 90}}\right)+s^{4}h_{p}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{w}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{w}+{{1\over 3}}h_{w}s^{2}T\lambda_{t}^{2}+s^{4}h_{w}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{3}+s^{2}\left({{4\over 9}}h_{3}T\lambda_{t}^{2}+{{h_{p}h_{3}\over 45}}\right)+s^{4}h_{3}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{4}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{4}+s^{2}\left({{2\over 3}}T\left(h_{4}-10\lambda_{p_{E}}^{2}\right)\lambda_{t}^{2}+{{h_{3}^{2}\over 270}}\right)+s^{4}h_{4}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{5}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{5}+{{1\over 270}}s^{2}\left(30T\lambda_{t}^{2}(6h_{p}+18h_{w}+5h_{5})+18h_{p}^{2}+81h_{w}^{2}+2h_{3}^{2}-1800T\lambda_{t}^{2}(\lambda_{p_{E}}^{2}+2\lambda_{t}^{2})\right)

\displaystyle+s^{4}h_{5}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{6}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{6}+s^{2}\left({{2\over 9}}\left(h_{3}+3h_{6}\right)T\lambda_{t}^{2}+{{h_{p}\over 45}}\left(h_{3}+2h_{6}\right)\right)+s^{4}h_{6}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{7}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{7}+{{s^{2}\over 270}}\left(\begin{multlined}30T\lambda_{t}^{2}(3h_{p}+9h_{w}+6h_{4}+6h_{5}+8h_{7})\\
\\
+9h_{p}^{2}+7h_{3}^{2}+12h_{3}h_{6}-1800T\lambda_{t}^{2}(\lambda_{t}^{2}+12\lambda_{p_{E}}\lambda_{d_{S}}+11\lambda_{p_{E}}^{2})\end{multlined}\right)

\displaystyle+s^{4}h_{7}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta\left(h_{8}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\epsilon h_{8}+{{s^{2}\over 270}}\left(\begin{multlined}30T\lambda_{t}^{2}(h_{5}+4h_{7}+12h_{8})+9h_{w}^{2}+2h_{3}^{2}+12h_{6}^{2}+12h_{3}h_{6}\\
\\
-1800T\lambda_{t}^{2}(24\lambda_{p_{E}}\lambda_{d_{S}}+18\lambda_{d_{S}}^{2}+5\lambda_{p_{E}}^{2})\end{multlined}\right)

\displaystyle+s^{4}h_{8}\left({{h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2}\over 5400}}-{{1\over 162}}T(11T+10)\lambda_{t}^{4}\right)+O(s^{5})

The general large-
N
anomalous dimensions are as given in
eqs.

18a
and
18b
. For completeness, we also give the beta functions for the masses (where necessarily they must now take non-zero values):

\displaystyle\beta(m^{2})

\displaystyle=-2m^{2}+{{4\over 9}}s^{2}T\left(m^{2}-3M^{2}\right)\lambda_{t}^{2}+8M\sqrt{m^{2}}s^{3}T\lambda_{t}^{2}\left(\lambda_{p_{E}}+\lambda_{d_{S}}\right)

(74a)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.19447pt+s^{4}\left({{2(h_{p}^{2}+3h_{w}^{2})m^{2}\over 2025}}+{{2\over 243}}T\lambda_{t}^{4}\left(63M^{2}(T+3)-m^{2}(5T+43)\right)\right)+O(s^{5})

\displaystyle\beta(M)

\displaystyle=-M+{{4\over 9}}Ms^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}+{{2M\over 243}}\lambda_{t}^{2}s^{4}\left(648T(\lambda_{d_{S}}+\lambda_{p_{E}})^{2}-13T\lambda_{t}^{2}-23\lambda_{t}^{2}\right)+O(s^{5})

(74b)

Note, that if we consider
\lambda\sim h\sim\epsilon
of the same magnitude, then these beta functions are only correct to cubic order in the coupling constants. To get the full quintic corrections it would be necessary to renormalise
h
using the
h^{5}
diagrams.

At the
\lambda_{\text{melonic}}
fixed point, we can use (
74b
) to compute the scaling dimensions of the mass operators; these match the non-perturbative calculations using
eqs.

63
and
64
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\phi^{2}}^{\text{pert}}

\displaystyle=1+\left(3-{{4\over T+1}}\right)\epsilon+{{2T\left(6T^{2}-22T-41\right)\over 3(T+1)^{3}}}\epsilon^{2}+O\left(\epsilon^{3}\right)=D+\derivative{\beta_{m^2}}{m^2}|_{fp}

(75a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{\psi}\psi}^{\text{pert}}

\displaystyle=2+\left({{4\over T+1}}-1\right)\epsilon-{{(4T(T+5)+42)\epsilon^{2}\over 3(T+1)^{3}}}+O\left(\epsilon^{3}\right)=D+\derivative{\beta_{M}}{M}|_{\lambda_{*}}

(75b)

B.3
Discrepancy with Jack and Poole

In
D=3
, we can transform each Dirac fermion into two Majorana fermions via
\psi_{a}={{1\over\sqrt{2}}}(\xi_{a,1}+i\xi_{a,2})
. This keeps the kinetic terms canonical, and modifies the interaction term from
{{1\over 2}}\lambda_{abcd}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}={{1\over 4}}Y_{abCD}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\bar{\xi}_{C}\xi_{D}
, with
Y_{abCD}=\lambda_{abcd}\delta_{x_{c}x_{d}}
, where
C,D
are superindices combining
c,d
and
x_{c,d}\in\{1,2\}
. Plugging this into the results of
[
16
]
, ignoring the gauge couplings, we find beta functions for
Y
and
h
. Though we have an alternate sign for
\lambda
, this does not change the beta functions. These should match ours when we substitute
T={{1\over 2}}T^{\prime}
in the equations of (
12
), since in 3D a single Majorana fermion is half of a Dirac fermion:

\displaystyle[\beta_{Y}]_{ab(c,x_{c})(d,x_{d})}=\beta_{\lambda_{abcd}}\delta_{x_{c},x_{d}}|_{T\to{{1\over 2}}T^{\prime}},\quad[\beta_{h}]_{abcdef}=[\beta_{h}]_{abcdef}|_{T\to{{1\over 2}}T^{\prime}}.

(76)

This is almost the case, except for the coefficient of
h_{B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}B_{4}B_{5}\nu}\lambda_{B_{6}\rho\tau\sigma}\lambda_{\nu\rho\sigma\tau}
. This matches up with the coefficient
d_{3}^{(2)}
of the tensor structure
V_{3}^{(2)}
. To match with our results as per (
76
), we need
d_{3}^{(2)}=T^{\prime}s^{2}
; the paper
[
16
]
has
d_{3}^{(2)}=2T^{\prime}s^{2}
(taking
T^{\prime}=2
). Since this term contributes to all of the
\beta_{h_{i}}
s in the melonic limit, the fact that the independent non-perturbative computations of this paper match our perturbative computations suggests that
d_{3}^{(2)}=T^{\prime}s^{2}
.

Appendix C
Vector model analysis

The general analysis can be reduced to the
\mathrm{O}(N_{b})\times\mathrm{U}(N_{f})
vector model, with
\phi_{a=1,\ldots,N_{b}}
,
\psi_{i=1,\ldots,N_{f}}
at finite
N_{b},N_{f}
, by keeping only the maximal-trace type couplings:

\displaystyle V_{\text{int}}(\phi,\psi)={{\lambda\over 2}}\phi_{a}\phi_{a}\bar{\psi}^{i}\psi_{i}+{{h\over 6!}}(\phi_{a}\phi_{a})^{2}.

(77)

In the case
N_{b}=N_{f}
, we could also permit the coupling
{{1\over 2}}\lambda_{d_{O}}(\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi_{i})(\phi_{j}\psi_{j})
, which, if our scalar was complex, would correspond to the Popović model studied in
appendix

D
– we will not do so here. Substituting into (
12
):

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon\lambda+{{8\over 3}}(TN_{f}+N_{b}+3)\lambda^{3}s^{2}+{{16\over 3}}{{(N_{b}+4)(N_{b}+2)\over 15}}{{h^{2}\over 15}}\lambda\,s^{4}+O(\lambda^{4},\ldots)

(78a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{h}&=-2\epsilon h+4(3N_{b}+22){{h^{2}\over 15}}s^{2}+32TN_{f}h\lambda^{2}s^{2}-720TN_{f}\lambda^{4}\\
&\quad\quad-{{1\over 2}}\left(\begin{multlined}\pi^{2}(((N_{b}+34)N_{b}+620)N_{b}+2720)\\
\\
+8((53N_{b}+858)N_{b}+3304)\end{multlined}\right){{h^{3}\over(15)^{2}}}s^{4}+O(h^{4},\lambda^{5},\ldots)\end{split}

(78b)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}

\displaystyle={{(N_{b}+2)(N_{b}+4)\over 15}}{{h^{2}\over 15}}{{s^{4}\over 6}}+{{1\over 3}}TN_{f}\lambda^{2}s^{2}+O(h^{3},\ldots)

(78c)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}

\displaystyle={{1\over 3}}N_{b}\lambda^{2}s^{2}+O(\lambda^{3},\ldots)

(78d)

For the bosonic sector, this agrees with the results of
[
29
]
, up to the identification
{{h\over 6!}}={{\pi^{2}\lambda\over 3}}
.

Thus for all
N_{b},N_{f},T
, six perturbative fixed points solve
\beta_{\lambda,h}=0
; to leading order in
\epsilon
, which manifestly match up in terms of
\epsilon
dependence to the prismatic-type fixed points that were identified in
section

3.3
:

\displaystyle\text{trivial}:\;

\displaystyle s\lambda=0,\;s^{2}h=0;

\displaystyle h_{\text{prismatic}}:\;

\displaystyle s\lambda=0,\;{{s^{2}h\over 15}}={{1\over 2(22+3N_{b})}}\epsilon;

\displaystyle h\lambda_{\text{prismatic}}:\;

\displaystyle s\lambda=\pm_{1}\left({{3\over 8(3+N_{b}+TN_{f})}}\right)^{{{1\over 2}}}\sqrt{\epsilon},

\displaystyle\qquad{{s^{2}h\over 15}}={{\left(1-{{6TN_{f}\over TN_{f}+N_{b}+3}}\pm_{2}\sqrt{{{3TN_{f}\left(8TN_{f}+23N_{b}+186\right)\over\left(TN_{f}+N_{b}+3\right){}^{2}}}+1}\right)\over 4(3N_{b}+22)}}\epsilon.

Thus, these prismatic theories have not only a large-
N
vector precursor, but also a finite-
N
precursor. The presence of the
h^{2}
and
\lambda^{4}
terms in
\beta_{h}
means that we are missing any precursors of the melonic fixed points; these are of course suppressed by the melonic limit.

C.1
Leading order conformal analysis

C.1.1
Free propagators

To set our conventions, we consider the following Lagrangians, in mostly positive signature, where
\bar{\psi}=\psi^{\dagger}\beta,\beta=i\gamma^{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\bar{\psi}(\not{\partial}+m)\psi,\quad\mathcal{L}=-{{1\over 2}}\phi(-\partial^{2}+m^{2})\phi

(79)

The free Lorentzian position-space propagator is
[
76
]
(where we set
m=0
):

G^{\phi}_{0}(x)=\expectationvalue{T \phi(x) \phi(0)}=\int{{d^{D}k\over(2\pi)^{D}}}{{-i\over k^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}}e^{+ik\cdot x}={{1\over(D-2)\Omega_{D-1}}}\left({{1\over x^{2}+i\epsilon}}\right)^{{{D-2\over 2}}}.

(80)

We define
\Omega_{x}
as the surface area of an
x
-sphere, i.e.
\Omega_{x}=2\pi^{(x+1)/2}/\Gamma((x+1)/2)
.
Once we know this, the fermion propagator is trivial:

\displaystyle G^{\psi}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue{T \psi(x) \bar{\psi}(0)}=\expectationvalue{T \psi_\alpha(x) \bar{\psi}_\beta(0)}=\int{{d^{D}k\over(2\pi)^{D}}}{{-i(-i\not{k}+m)\over k^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}}e^{+ik\cdot x}

(81)

\displaystyle=\int_{k}{{-i(-\not\partial+m)\over k^{2}+m^{2}-i\epsilon}}e^{+ik\cdot x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m=0}}{{=}}-\not{\partial}G_{0}^{\phi}(x)={{\not{x}\over\Omega_{D-1}}}\left({{1\over x^{2}+i\epsilon}}\right)^{{{D\over 2}}}

C.1.2
Anomalous dimensions

Since we are interested in the fixed points of (
3
), we perform a simple conformal analysis in
D=3-\epsilon
. The equations of motion are

\displaystyle\Box\phi=\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi=\lambda\phi\bar{\psi}\psi+{{h\over 5!}}\phi^{5}

(82)

\displaystyle\left(\not{\partial}+{{\lambda\over 2}}\phi^{2}\right)\psi=0,\quad\bar{\psi}\left(-\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}+{{\lambda\over 2}}\phi^{2}\right)=0.

(83)

Considered as operator equations, these must be normal ordered. However, at the conformal fixed point, we know that the propagator looks like

\displaystyle G(x)=\kappa_{\text{full}}\left({{1\over x^{2}+i\epsilon}}\right)^{(D-2)/2+\gamma_{\phi}}=G_{0}^{\phi}(x)+O(\gamma_{\phi})

(84)

for some constant
\kappa_{\text{full}}
. At precisely
D=3
, we know that the theory is free, and hence near
D=3
we can assume that
\gamma_{\phi}
,
\lambda
, and
h
are small (at least
O(\sqrt{\epsilon})
), as with the usual Wilson-Fisher fixed point. Squaring (
82
), we can then calculate the following quantity
15
15

15

We use
\partial_{\mu}(1/\absolutevalue{x}^{a})=-ax^{\mu}/\absolutevalue{x}^{a+2}
, which gives
\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(1/\absolutevalue{x}^{a})=a(a+2-D)/\absolutevalue{x}^{a+2}
:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{:\Box{\phi}(x)::\Box{\phi}(0):}

\displaystyle=\Box^{2}G(x)|_{x=0}={{4\over(D-2)\Omega_{D-1}}}(D-2)D\gamma_{\phi}\left({{1\over x^{2}+i\epsilon}}\right)^{(D+2)/2}+O(\gamma_{\phi}^{2})

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue{:\lambda\phi\bar{\psi}\psi+\frac{h}{5!} \phi^5:|_{x} :\lambda\phi\bar{\psi}\psi+ \frac{h}{5!} \phi^5|_{0}:}

We can assume the propagators are free to this order, and therefore we obtain

\displaystyle{{12\over\Omega_{2}}}\gamma_{\phi}\left({{1\over x^{2}+i\epsilon}}\right)^{5/2}+O(\gamma_{\phi}^{2})=\lambda^{2}\expectationvalue{:\phi_x\phi_0:}\expectationvalue{:\bar{\psi}\psi_x::\bar{\psi}\psi_0:}+{{h^{2}\over(5!)^{2}}}\expectationvalue{:\phi_x^5: :: \phi_0^5:}.

(85)

Precisely the same calculation can be performed for the fermionic equation of motion. From the free theory, we know
\kappa_{\text{full}}=(\Omega_{2})^{-1}=2s+O(\epsilon)
, where
s=1/(8\pi)
as usual. So performing the free Wick contractions, we obtain that to leading order in
\epsilon
, with
\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\mathbb{I}_{s}]=T
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}={{1\over 3}}T\lambda^{2}s^{2}+{{4\over 3}}{{h^{2}\over 5!}},\quad\gamma_{\psi}={{\lambda^{2}\over 3}}s^{2}.

(86)

This is trivially extensible to the general vector case, which yields precisely
eqs.

12c
and
12d
. In the case of
N_{f}
fermions and
N_{b}
scalars, satisfying an
\mathrm{O}(N_{b})\times\mathrm{U}(N_{f})
symmetry, adding the appropriate
\delta_{ij}
and
\delta^{m}_{n}
s to the Lagrangian we find exactly
eqs.

78c
and
78d
.

C.1.3
Beta functions

As demonstrated by Cardy
[
77
,
78
]
, we can use conformal perturbation theory
[
79
]
(see also section 4 of
[
23
]
) to obtain the quadratic terms in the
\beta_{h}
, in a particular scheme, using the OPE coefficients
C^{I}_{JK}
of the CFT about which we are perturbing. Specifically, if perturbing a CFT with the set of dimensionless couplings
S_{\text{int}}=\sum_{I}\lambda^{I}\mu^{D-\Delta_{I}}\int\mathcal{O}_{I}
, their beta functions should be

\displaystyle\beta^{I}=(\Delta_{I}-D)\lambda^{I}+{{1\over 2}}\Omega_{D-1}C^{I}_{JK}\lambda^{J}\lambda^{K}+O(\lambda^{3}),

(87)

where
C^{I}_{JK}
is the OPE coefficient in the conformal theory:
\mathcal{O}_{J}\times\mathcal{O}_{K}\sim\sum_{I}C^{I}_{JK}\mathcal{O}_{I}
. The particular scheme is similar to but not precisely the same as MS scheme; however, to this order there is no difference in the beta function, because we are only considering the marginal couplings and MS scheme is massless. For our case, take
\lambda^{I}=h
, with
\mathcal{O}_{I}=\phi^{6}/6!
as the operator in the Gaussian CFT. From the Gaussian OPE, it is easy to calculate that the OPE coefficient
C^{h}_{hh}
is
{6\choose 3}\times 6\times 5\times 4=2400
multiplied by terms coming from the normalisation of
h
and
\phi
:

\displaystyle{{\phi^{6}(x)\over 6!}}\times{{\phi^{6}(y)\over 6!}}\supset 2400\times{{1\over 6!}}G_{0}(x-y)^{3}{{\phi^{6}(y)\over 6!}}+\cdots,

(88)

where
G_{0}^{\phi}(x)
is the usual free propagator, which in mostly positive or positive signature is (
80
). Hence, we find that the coefficient of the
h^{2}
term is indeed as given in (
12
)

\displaystyle{{1\over 2}}\Omega_{D-1}C^{h}_{hh}=\left[{{1\over 2}}\Omega_{D-1}\times{{2400\over 6!}}\times\left({{1\over(D-2)\Omega_{D-1}}}\right)^{3}\right]_{D=3}={{20\over 3}}s^{2}.

(89)

Appendix D
Analysis of the Popović model

Here we resolve some unclear points with the original model of
[
13
]
, which is the large-
N
theory of two
N
-vector fields, a Dirac fermion and a complex scalar. The corrected
16
16

16

The paper gives Lagrangian containing “
{{1\over 2}}\phi^{\dagger}\phi
”, but the
\phi
field is clearly intended to be complex (for example, using for the one-boson-loop correction to the
\phi
propagator a symmetry factor of 1). They use
T=\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\mathbb{I}_{s}]=2
, due to working in exactly
D=3
– we will leave
T
general here for clarity.

\mathrm{U}(N)
-symmetric Lagrangian there corresponds to our model but with a complex
\phi
and the only non-zero coupling being
\lambda_{d_{O}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\phi_{i}^{\dagger}(-\partial^{2}+m_{0}^{2})\phi_{i}+\bar{\psi}_{i}(i\gamma_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}-M_{0})\psi_{i}+g_{0}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}(\bar{\psi}_{i}\phi_{i}^{\dagger})(\phi_{j}\psi_{j}).

(90)

We stress that the metric signature in
[
13
]
is
(+--)
, and take

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\mathbb{I}_{s}]

\displaystyle=T,\quad\phi_{i=1,\ldots,N},\quad\psi_{i=1,\ldots,N}.

(91)

They analyse this theory in the large-
N
limit, finding that the beta function is zero in exactly three spacetime dimensions. Beginning with the four-point vertex function

\displaystyle ig_{0}\Gamma_{4}(p)={{ig_{0}\over 1-ig_{0}\tilde{\Sigma}(p)}}={{1\over 1-Ng_{0}G\not{p}(-p^{2})^{(D-4)/2}}},\quad G\equiv{{\Gamma({{4-D\over 2}})B({{D\over 2}},{{D-2\over 2}})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}}

(92)

We define the renormalised and rescaled coupling
g
by

\displaystyle g_{0}N

\displaystyle\equiv Z_{g}Z_{\phi}Z_{\psi}g\mu^{3-D}=Z_{g}g\mu^{3-D}+O(1/N),

(93)

\displaystyle ig

\displaystyle\equiv ig_{0}[\Gamma_{4}(p^{2}=\mu^{2})]_{\mathbb{I}},

where
[\cdot]_{\mathbb{I}}
indicates that we drop the part proportional to
\not{p}
, and we have used the fact that the fields are not renormalised to leading order. This gives that
Z_{g}=1+g_{0}^{2}N^{2}G^{2}\mu^{2(D-3)}+O(1/N)=1+Z_{g}^{2}g^{2}G^{2}+O(1/N)
, and so almost every value of the renormalised coupling in the allowed range
g\in[-1/(2G),+1/(2G)]
can be reached by two values of the bare coupling (which can take any real value):

\displaystyle Z_{g}

\displaystyle={{1\pm\sqrt{1-4g^{2}G^{2}}\over 2g^{2}G^{2}}}+O(1/N)

(94)

\displaystyle\implies\beta_{g}

\displaystyle=\mu{{\mathrm{d}g\over\mathrm{d}g}}=\pm(3-D)\sqrt{1-4g^{2}G^{2}}+O(1/N)

Note that in the limit
D=4-\epsilon
,
G\propto 1/\epsilon
, so this theory has a Wilson-Fisher-like fixed point that is perturbative around
D=4
. They then move on to calculate the anomalous dimension of
\phi
. However, in their equation (3.4) the
{{1\over\sqrt{-k^{2}}}}
should be a
{{1\over(-k^{2})^{(D-2)/2}}}
– they have taken the limit too early. However, in trying to resolve this, we then have to evaluate the integral

\displaystyle i\tilde{\Pi}(p^{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits\int{{d^{D}k\over(2\pi)^{D}}}{{i\over\cancel{(k-p)}}}(ig_{0})i\Gamma_{4}(k)=ig_{0}\int_{k}{{1\over(k-p)^{2}}}\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits\left[\cancel{(k-p)}\Gamma_{4}(k)\right]

(95)

\displaystyle=-iTg_{0}\int_{k}{{(k-p)\cdot k\over[-(k-p)^{2}]^{1}}}{{g_{0}NG(-k^{2})^{{{D-4\over 2}}}\over 1+g_{0}^{2}N^{2}G^{2}(-k^{2})^{D-3}}}

\displaystyle\propto\int dqq^{D-1}{{q^{2}\over q^{2}}}{{q^{D-4}\over q^{2D-6}}}\sim\int_{0}^{\infty}dqq^{D-1}{{1\over q^{D-2}}}\to\infty,

which is manifestly divergent for all values of
D
. A neat way to proceed in precisely
D=3
is to regulate by consistently shifting the conformal scaling dimension of the Hubbard-Stratonovich field
[
80
]
; that is, we switch
(-k^{2})^{{{D-4\over 2}}}|_{D=3}\to(-k^{2})^{-{{1\over 2}}-\eta}\mu^{2\eta}
, for some infinitesimal
\eta
, which becomes the regulator:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}(p^{2})|_{D=3}

\displaystyle=-T{{g_{0}^{2}NG\over 1+g_{0}^{2}N^{2}G^{2}}}\int_{k}{{(k-p)\cdot k\over[-(k-p)^{2}]^{1}(-k^{2})^{{{1\over 2}}+\eta}}}

(96)

\displaystyle={{-i(-p^{2})^{1-\eta}\over\eta}}T{{\tilde{A}\over N}}\times\mu^{2\eta}+O(\eta^{0}),

\displaystyle\tilde{A}

\displaystyle\equiv{{1\over 12\pi^{2}}}{{(Ng_{0})^{2}G\over 1+(Ng_{0}G)^{2}}}={{4\over 3\pi^{2}}}{{Z_{g}-1\over Z_{g}}}

where
T\tilde{A}=A_{\text{Popovi\'{c}}}
. Then we can define the field renormalisation
Z_{\phi}\phi_{R}\equiv\phi
with the renormalisation condition
\Delta_{R}(p^{2})={{i\over p^{2}}}
at
p^{2}=-\mu^{2}
. This gives
Z_{\phi}=(1+{{T\tilde{A}\over N}}{{1\over\eta}})^{-1}
, and so the anomalous dimension is simply
\gamma_{\phi}={{1\over 2}}\derivative{\log Z_\phi^2}{\log\mu}=T{{\tilde{A}\over N}}+O(1/N^{2})
.
Playing the same game for the fermion, we find an anomalous dimension
\gamma_{\phi}=N_{b}{{\tilde{A}\over N}}+O(1/N^{2})
, where we define
N_{b}=2
as the number of real degrees of freedom of each entry of the scalar field. These results agree with those of
[
13
]
. In the strong coupling limit,
g_{0}N\to\infty
,
Z_{g}\to\infty
,
g\to 0
these anomalous dimensions also precisely match the leading order results of a
\phi_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\lambda
-type melonic theory in
D=3

[
40
]
, where
\lambda
is a fermionic Hubbard-Stratonovich field. We note that with canonical kinetic terms, the IR scaling for this melonic theory (
\gamma_{\phi},\gamma_{\psi}>0,\gamma_{\lambda}>0
) is in fact only consistent for
2<D<3
; in particular, the requirement
\gamma_{\lambda}\geq 0
invalidates these solutions for
D\geq 3
.

Appendix E
SDE Euclidean integral calculations in general D

For ease of use, we put here some standard Euclidean integrals, which are calculated in a straightforward fashion described in
Appendix B
of
[
81
]
.

The first is symmetric in
\alpha\longleftrightarrow\beta
, which we emphasise by defining
X_{2}\equiv D-\alpha-\beta
.

\displaystyle I_{\alpha,\beta}(p)

\displaystyle=\int{{\mathrm{d}^{D}k\over(2\pi)^{D}}}{{1\over(k^{2})^{\alpha}((k+p)^{2})^{\beta}}}={{1\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}}{{\Gamma\left({{D\over 2}}-\alpha\right)\Gamma\left({{D\over 2}}-\beta\right)\Gamma\left(\alpha+\beta-{{D\over 2}}\right)\over\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(D-\alpha-\beta)(p^{2})^{\alpha+\beta-{{D\over 2}}}}}

(97)

\displaystyle={{1\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}}{{1\over(p^{2})^{{{D\over 2}}-X_{2}}}}{{\Gamma\left({{D\over 2}}-\alpha\right)\Gamma\left({{D\over 2}}-\beta\right)\Gamma\left({{D\over 2}}-X_{2}\right)\over\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(X_{2})}}.

Another basic integral is

\displaystyle\int_{k}{{k\cdot p\over k^{2\alpha}(k+p)^{2\beta}}}

\displaystyle={{1\over 2}}\int_{k}{{(k+p)^{2}-k^{2}-p^{2}\over k^{2\alpha}(k+p)^{2\beta}}}={{1\over 2}}\left(I_{\alpha,\beta-1}(p)-I_{\alpha-1,\beta}(p)-p^{2}I_{\alpha,\beta}(p)\right).

(98)

Next, the quartic bosonic melon, symmetric in
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, with
X_{3}\equiv{{3D\over 2}}-\alpha-\beta-\gamma
:

\displaystyle I_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\int_{k,l}{{1\over(k+l+p)^{2\alpha}k^{2\beta}l^{2\gamma}}}={{1\over(4\pi)^{D}}}{{\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-\alpha)\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-\beta)\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-\gamma)\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-X_{3})\over\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(X_{3})(p^{2})^{{{D\over 2}}-X-3}}},

(99a)

which has similar variations

\displaystyle J_{\alpha\beta\gamma}(p)=\int_{k,l}{{k\cdot p\over(k+l+p)^{2\alpha}k^{2\beta}l^{2\gamma}}}={{-(D-2\beta)p^{2}\over 3D-2(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)}}I_{\alpha\beta\gamma}(p),

(99b)

\displaystyle K_{\alpha\beta\gamma}(p)=\int_{k,l}{{k\cdot l\over(k+l+p)^{2\alpha}k^{2\beta}l^{2\gamma}}}={{-(D-2\beta)(D-2\gamma)p^{2}\,I_{\alpha\beta\gamma}(p)\over 2(\alpha+\beta+\gamma-D-1)(3D-2(\alpha+\beta+\gamma))}}.

(99c)

Hence, using that integrals containing
\not{k}
or
\not{l}
must be proportional to
\not{p}
, we find:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{F}^{FBB}(p)

\displaystyle={{\lambda_{t}^{2}\over 2}}fb^{2}\left(I_{\alpha\beta\beta}+{{1\over p^{2}}}(J_{\alpha\beta\beta}+J_{\alpha\beta\beta})\right),

(100a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{B}^{BBB}(p)

\displaystyle={{g_{t}^{2}\over 3!}}b^{3}I_{\beta\beta\beta},

(100b)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{B}^{BFF}(p)

\displaystyle=-{{\lambda_{t}^{2}\over 1}}bf^{2}K_{\beta\alpha\alpha}\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits[\mathbb{I}_{s}].

(100c)

We also require the sextic bosonic melon, which in Euclidean signature is
[
5
]
:

\Sigma_{B}^{B^{5}}(p)={{h_{t}^{2}b^{5}\over 5!}}\left({{p^{4D-10}\over(4\pi)^{2D}}}{{\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-b)^{5}\Gamma(5b-2D)\over\Gamma(b)^{5}\Gamma(5{{D\over 2}}-5b)}}\right).

(101)

E.1
Euclidean integrals for q-model

The general Euclidean scalar melon with
q-1
different arbitrary conformal propagators
\sim p^{-2\alpha_{i}}
is found by induction to be:

\displaystyle I_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q-1}}(p)=\int\prod_{i=1}^{q-2}\left({{d^{d}k_{i}\over(2\pi)^{d}}}{{1\over k_{i}^{2\alpha_{i}}}}\right){{1\over(\sum_{i}^{q-2}k_{i}+p)^{2\alpha_{q-1}}}}

(102a)

\displaystyle={{1\over(4\pi)^{D(q-2)/2}}}\left(\prod_{i}^{q-1}{{\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-\alpha_{i})\over\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}}\right){{\Gamma({{D\over 2}}-\sum_{i}^{q-1}({{D\over 2}}-\alpha_{i}))\over\Gamma(\sum_{i}^{q-1}({{D\over 2}}-\alpha_{i}))}}{{1\over p^{2({{D\over 2}}-\sum_{i}^{q-1}({{D\over 2}}-\alpha_{i}))}}}

The induction proceeds by noting that
I_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q-1}}(p)=\int_{k}k^{-2\alpha_{q-1}}I_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q-2}}(p+k)
; the base case and inductive step are both (
97
). The fermion integrals, with numerator
k\cdot l
, can also be found by induction from the base case, with the result:

\displaystyle K_{mel}(p,\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\},\{\alpha_{3},\ldots,\alpha_{q-1}\})

(102b)

\displaystyle=\int{{d^{d}k_{1}\over(2\pi)^{d}}}{{d^{d}k_{2}\over(2\pi)^{d}}}{{k_{1}\cdot k_{2}\over k_{1}^{2\alpha_{1}+1}k_{2}^{2\alpha_{2}+1}}}\,\prod_{i=3}^{q-2}\left({{d^{d}k_{i}\over(2\pi)^{d}}}{{1\over k_{i}^{2\alpha_{i}}}}\right){{1\over(\sum_{i=1}^{q-2}k_{i}+p)^{2\alpha_{q-1}}}}

\displaystyle={{-1\over(4\pi)^{D(q-2)/2}}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2}{{\Gamma(D/2-\alpha_{i}+1/2)\over\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1/2)}}\right)\left(\prod_{i=3}^{q-1}{{\Gamma(D/2-\alpha_{i})\over\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}}\right)

\displaystyle\times{{\Gamma(D/2-\sum_{i}^{q-1}(D/2-\alpha_{i}))\over\Gamma(\sum_{i}^{q-1}(D/2-\alpha_{i}))}}{{1\over p^{2(D/2-\sum_{i}^{q-1}(D/2-\alpha_{i}))}}}

We can work out the propagators with
k\cdot p
in the same way:

\displaystyle J_{mel}(p,\{\alpha_{1}\},\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q-1}\})

(102c)

\displaystyle=\int{{d^{d}k_{1}\over(2\pi)^{d}}}{{k_{1}\cdot p\over k_{1}^{2\alpha_{1}+1}}}\,\prod_{i=2}^{q-2}\left({{d^{d}k_{i}\over(2\pi)^{d}}}{{1\over k_{i}^{2\alpha_{i}}}}\right){{1\over(\sum_{i=1}^{q-2}k_{i}+p)^{2\alpha_{q-1}}}}

\displaystyle={{-1\over(4\pi)^{D(q-2)/2}}}\left({{\Gamma(D/2-\alpha_{1}+1/2)\over\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+1/2)}}\right)\left(\prod_{i=2}^{q-1}{{\Gamma(D/2-\alpha_{i})\over\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}}\right)

\displaystyle\times{{\Gamma(D/2+1/2-\sum_{i}^{q-1}(D/2-\alpha_{i}))\over\Gamma(1/2+\sum_{i}^{q-1}(D/2-\alpha_{i}))}}{{1\over p^{2(D/2-1/2-\sum_{i}^{q-1}(D/2-\alpha_{i}))}}}
